 DDI → \rightarrow CE 
 ? 
 NS + CO/ONeMg WD 

 ≳ 10 \gtrsim 10 
 CE 
 no 
 DNS 

 Notes — The division between IMXBs and HMXBs is somewhat unclear but typically at a donor star mass of M 2 ≃ 8 − 10 ​ M ⊙ M_{2}\simeq 8-10\;M_{\odot} .
A DNS system is only produced from a HMXB evolving through a CE phase if the initial orbital period is wide enough (see Section 3.4.2). 

We have recently been notified that optical monitoring of the NS ULX system NGC 7793 P13 ( Motch et al. 2014 ) 
has revealed a ∼ 20 ​ M ⊙ \sim 20\;M_{\odot} donor star and an orbital period of ∼ 64 ​ days \sim 64\;{\rm days} . If this interpretation is correct, it is a very puzzling
result and it challenges current understanding of long-term stability of such HMXBs (unless this source initiated RLO very
recently and was fortunate to be discovered in the short time span before it becomes dynamically unstable). If the system in
the future evolves through a CE phase, then we expect it to coalesce and produce a TŻO, as explained in Section 3.4). 

To summarize our analysis on DNS progenitor systems in this section, we conclude that only wide-orbit HMXBs (possibly only a few, if any,
of the observed Galactic HMXBs) will eventually survive the CE evolution and potentially produce DNS systems.
NS ULX systems are possibly IMXBs and, if so, not massive enough to be progenitors of DNS systems. 

### IV. Amount of mass accreted by recycled pulsars in DNS systems

A first estimate of the amount of matter accreted by recycled pulsars in different types of binary pulsar systems was made by Taam & van den Heuvel 1986 .
Here, to infer the birth masses of the first-born NSs in DNS systems (and allow for direct comparison with the masses of the second-born NSs, or NSs in other binaries like HMXBs),
we have identified five consecutive phases of evolution where the first-born NS in a DNS system will potentially accrete material (cf. Fig.  1 ): 

 i) 

wind accretion in the HMXB stage; 

 ii) 

CE and spiral-in evolution; 

 iii) 

wind accretion from the helium/Wolf-Rayet star; 

 iv) 

Case BB Roche-lobe overflow; 

 v) 

shell impact from the SN of the secondary star. 

We now discuss the expected amount of matter accreted by the NS in each of these five phases. 

### IV.1. Wind accretion in the HMXB stage

The stellar wind mass-loss rate of early type stars is subject to large uncertainties.
The observational and theoretical estimated values depend on the modelling of the terminal wind velocity, clumping effects, metallicity
and absorption properties in the local environment ( Kudritzki & Puls 2000 ; Vink et al. 2001 ; Repolust et al. 2004 ; Crowther et al. 2006 ; Mokiem et al. 2007 ; Markova & Puls 2008 ; Langer 2012 ; Šurlan et al. 2013 ; Chaty 2013 ; Falanga et al. 2015 ) .
In the following, we shall begin by assuming a wind mass-loss rate of a typical OB-star companion in a HMXB of
 | M ˙ 2 , wind | ≃ 10 − 6 − 10 − 5 ​ M ⊙ ​ yr − 1 |\dot{M}_{\rm 2,wind}|\simeq 10^{-6}-10^{-5}\;M_{\odot}\,{\rm yr}^{-1} (assuming that the effect of X-ray irradiation on the
wind mass-loss rate of the companion star is negligible).
The wind velocity can be approximated by the escape velocity at the stellar surface: v wind ≃ v esc = 2 ​ G ​ M 2 / R 2 v_{\rm wind}\simeq v_{\rm esc}=\sqrt{2GM_{2}/R_{2}} ,
where M 2 M_{2} and R 2 R_{2} are the companion star mass and radius, and G G is the constant of gravity.
Moreover, we assume Bondi-Hoyle-like accretion to be valid, given the highly supersonic flow of the stellar wind from the companion star.
The gravitational capture radius of the accreting NS is then given by: R acc = 2 ​ G ​ M NS / ( v rel 2 + c s 2 ) R_{\rm acc}=2GM_{\rm NS}/(v_{\rm rel}^{2}+c_{s}^{2}) ,
where c s = γ ​ P / ρ ≈ 11 ​ T / ( 10 4 ​ K ) ​ km ​ s − 1 c_{s}=\sqrt{\gamma P/\rho}\approx 11\,\sqrt{T/(10^{4}\,K)}\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} is the local sound speed of the
ambient medium ( γ \gamma is the adiabatic index, P P is the pressure, ρ \rho is the mass density and
 T T is the temperature).
The relative velocity between the wind and the NS ( v → rel = v → wind + v → orb \vec{v}_{\rm rel}=\vec{v}_{\rm wind}+\vec{v}_{\rm orb} ) is usually dominated
by the wind velocity compared to the orbital velocity, i.e. v wind ≫ v orb v_{\rm wind}\gg v_{\rm orb} (except for very tight, and/or highly eccentric binaries).
Similarly, we have v wind ≫ c s v_{\rm wind}\gg c_{s} .
The resulting accretion rate onto the NS, M ˙ NS ≈ π ​ R acc 2 ​ ρ ​ v wind \dot{M}_{\rm NS}\approx\pi R_{\rm acc}^{2}\rho\,v_{\rm wind} 
can be combined with the continuity equation: ρ = | M ˙ 2 , wind | / ( 4 ​ π ​ a 2 ​ v wind ) \rho=|\dot{M}_{\rm 2,wind}|/(4\pi a^{2}\,v_{\rm wind}) 
to yield: 

 M ˙ NS ≈ ( G ​ M NS ) 2 ​ | M ˙ 2 , wind | a 2 ​ v wind 4 , \dot{M}_{\rm NS}\approx\frac{(GM_{\rm NS})^{2}\,|\dot{M}_{\rm 2,wind}|}{a^{2}v_{\rm wind}^{4}}, 

 (3) 

where a a is the orbital separation.
An estimate from this equation indicates that, in wind-driven scenarios, M ˙ NS \dot{M}_{\rm NS} is typically
 10 3 − 10 4 10^{3}-10^{4} times lower than | M ˙ 2 , wind | |\dot{M}_{\rm 2,wind}| (for helium-star winds the ratio is even lower
by an order of magnitude, cf. Section  IV.3 ).
Hence, we adopt a typical NS accretion rate of M ˙ NS ≈ 10 − 9 ​ M ⊙ ​ yr − 1 \dot{M}_{\rm NS}\approx 10^{-9}\;M_{\odot}\,{\rm yr}^{-1} ,
corresponding to an X-ray luminosity of the order, L x ≈ 10 37 ​ erg ​ s − 1 L_{x}\approx 10^{37}\;{\rm erg\,s^{-1}} . 

To estimate the amount of material accreted by the NS during wind accretion in the HMXB stage,
we then simply multiply this rate with the expected lifetime of the HMXB companion star during
which NS wind accretion is active: Δ ​ M NS = M ˙ NS ⋅ Δ ​ t 2 {\Delta M}_{\rm NS}=\dot{M}_{\rm NS}\cdot\Delta t_{2} .
However, an analysis of the X-ray luminosity function of Galactic HMXBs ( Grimm et al. 2002 ) shows
that only ∼ \sim\! 10% of the sources have L x ≥ 10 37 ​ erg ​ s − 1 L_{x}\geq 10^{37}\;{\rm erg\,s^{-1}} .
This fact probably reflects a limited amount of time in which a HMXB companion star is able
to deliver a sufficiently high wind mass-loss rate and/or our initially assumed (average) wind mass-loss rate, | M ˙ 2 , wind | |\dot{M}_{\rm 2,wind}| is too large.
During most of the (main sequence) lifetime of the HMXB donor star, the value of | M ˙ 2 , wind | |\dot{M}_{\rm 2,wind}| is often lower
than 10 − 6 ​ M ⊙ ​ yr − 1 10^{-6}\;M_{\odot}\,{\rm yr}^{-1} .
Indeed, from a study of 10 eclipsing HMXBs, Falanga et al. 2015 list typical values of | M ˙ 2 , wind | ≈ 10 − 7 − 10 − 6 ​ M ⊙ ​ yr − 1 |\dot{M}_{\rm 2,wind}|\approx 10^{-7}-10^{-6}\;M_{\odot}\,{\rm yr}^{-1} ,
based on observations of their average NS accretion rates.
Hence, by adopting a value of Δ ​ t 2 ∼ 10 7 ​ yr \Delta t_{2}\sim 10^{7}\;{\rm yr} and slightly smaller values of | M ˙ 2 , wind | |\dot{M}_{\rm 2,wind}| and M ˙ NS \dot{M}_{\rm NS} , we finally obtain
 Δ ​ M NS ≈ a ​ few × 10 − 3 ​ M ⊙ {\Delta M}_{\rm NS}\approx{\rm a~few}\,\times 10^{-3}\;M_{\odot} for wind accretion during the HMXB phase. 

The structured nature of the stellar wind of a supergiant star (i.e. a wind where cool dense clumps are embedded in a photoionized gas)
makes it difficult to derive an estimate of its mass-loss rate that is more accurate than a factor of a few ( Martínez-Núñez
et al. 2017 , e.g.) .
Another caveat is that the presence of a NS causes hydrodynamical interactions of the wind flow in close binaries ( Manousakis et al. 2012 ) .
However, it is also possible that a strong reduction of the long-term mass-accretion rate is produced by the effect of
a strong magnetic field and/or a fast spin of the NS, which could lead to magnetic and/or centrifugal barriers, respectively ( Bozzo et al. 2008 ; Bozzo et al. 2016 ) .
Such complications are not taken into account in our simplified Bondi-Hoyle-like treatment of the accretion process (see also Section  IV.6 ). 

### IV.2. CE and spiral-in evolution

The question of accretion onto a NS undergoing spiral-in during a CE
event has been a subject of long debate. From a theoretical point of view,
it has been suggested that a NS in a CE might suffer from hypercritical accretion ( Chevalier 1993 ) 
leading to its collapse into a BH. Thus to explain the observed DNS systems, an alternative
scenario without CE evolution was invented: the double core scenario ( Brown 1995 ; Dewi et al. 2006 ) .
In this scenario, two stars with an initial mass ratio close to unity evolve in parallel and reach
the giant stage roughly at the same time. Therefore, when the CE forms it will embed both stars in their giant stages (or as a giant star and a helium core)
thereby avoiding the formation of a CE with a NS.
This scenario, however, only works for the evolution of two stars with a mass ratio close to 1
and thus cannot explain the observations of tight binaries with, for example, a NS orbited by a WD companion, e.g. PSR B0655+64 ( Damashek et al. 1982 ; Kulkarni 1986 ) ,
PSR J1802 − - 2124 ( Ferdman et al. 2010 ) and PSR J1952+2630 ( Lazarus et al. 2014 ) .
Such systems show clear evidence of having evolved through a stage with a NS embedded in a CE ( van den Heuvel & Taam 1984 ; Tauris et al. 2012 ) . 

Recent theoretical works by MacLeod & Ramirez-Ruiz 2015a ; MacLeod & Ramirez-Ruiz 2015b seem to resolve this paradox. From their hydrodynamical simulations they find that
a NS embedded in a CE only accretes a very modest amount of material during its spiral-in as a result of a density gradient across its
accretion radius, which strongly limits accretion by imposing a net angular momentum to the flow around the NS.
 MacLeod & Ramirez-Ruiz 2015b find an upper limit of Δ ​ M NS < 0.1 ​ M ⊙ \Delta M_{\rm NS}<0.1\;M_{\odot} . However, they remark in their paper that they
 make several approximations that likely lead their calculations of accreted mass in a CE to be an overestimate ,
including an assumption of a static structure for the CE, effective hypercritical accretion and cooling by neutrinos, as well as
propagation of accreted mass all the way down to the shock radius. 

Observational support for such a small amount of NS accretion during the CE phase is
given by the four DNS systems (Table  2 ) in which the first-born NS (the recycled component) has a mass of
less than 1.38 ​ M ⊙ 1.38\;M_{\odot} , and also PSR J1802 − - 2124 where the NS mass is estimated to be ∼ 1.24 ± 0.11 ​ M ⊙ \sim\!1.24\pm 0.11\;M_{\odot} ( Ferdman et al. 2010 ) .
If all these NSs had accreted of the order 0.1 ​ M ⊙ 0.1\;M_{\odot} they would need to have been born with a mass of
 M NS < 1.28 ​ M ⊙ M_{\rm NS}<1.28\;M_{\odot} .
While this is still possible, it would be unexpected for the mass of the first-born NS in so many systems.
Furthermore, the NS is also expected to accrete material in the subsequent phases discussed below, which would require even
smaller birth masses in these cases.
In the following, we take Δ ​ M NS = 0.01 ​ M ⊙ {\Delta M}_{\rm NS}=0.01\;M_{\odot} as a reasonable estimate for the upper limit of the amount of mass accreted by
a NS during a CE phase, although we cannot fully rule out the possibility for further accretion. 

### IV.3. Wind accretion from the helium/Wolf-Rayet star 

The recipe for calculating the amount of mass accreted by the NS from the fast wind of its naked helium (or Wolf-Rayet, WR) star companion
is similar to that given in Section  IV.1 , i.e. Δ ​ M NS ≃ | M ˙ He , wind | ⋅ f acc ⋅ Δ ​ t He \Delta M_{\rm NS}\simeq|\dot{M}_{\rm He,wind}|\cdot f_{\rm acc}\cdot\Delta t_{\rm He} ,
where Δ ​ t He \Delta t_{\rm He} is the lifetime of the helium star, | M ˙ He , wind | |\dot{M}_{\rm He,wind}| is its mass-loss rate, and
 f acc = M ˙ NS / | M ˙ He , wind | f_{\rm acc}=\dot{M}_{\rm NS}/|\dot{M}_{\rm He,wind}| is the wind accretion efficiency.
It should be noted that helium stars with mass ≲ 8 ​ M ⊙ \lesssim 8\;M_{\odot} are never seen as WR stars, meaning that the strong WR winds
occur only in helium stars ≳ 8 ​ M ⊙ \gtrsim 8\;M_{\odot} ( Crowther 2007 ) . Such massive helium stars, however, rarely end up as progenitors of DNS systems.
From stellar evolution modelling of NS–helium star binaries ( Tauris et al. 2015 ) , we find that typically
 f acc ≃ a ​ few ×  10 − 4 f_{\rm acc}\simeq{\rm a~few~}\times\;10^{-4} ( Israel 1996 , see also) ,
and we obtain values of Δ ​ M NS < 4 × 10 − 4 ​ M ⊙ \Delta M_{\rm NS}<4\times 10^{-4}\;M_{\odot} when integrating throughout the
wind-accretion phase of the NS–helium star binaries. 

### IV.4. Case BB Roche-lobe overflow

The amount of material which can be accreted by the NS as a result of Case BB RLO is limited
by the short duration of this mass-transfer phase ( < 10 5 ​ yr <10^{5}\;{\rm yr} ) combined
with an Eddington-limited accretion rate onto the NS, which is roughly given by (cgs units in the central term): 

 M ˙ Edd = 4 ​ π ​ c ​ R NS 0.2 ​ ( 1 + X ) ≃  3.0 × 10 − 8 ​ M ⊙ ​ yr − 1 , \dot{M}_{\rm Edd}=\frac{4\pi c\,R_{\rm NS}}{0.2\,(1+X)}\;\simeq\;3.0\times 10^{-8}\;M_{\odot}\,{\rm yr}^{-1}\,, 

 (4) 

for accretion of helium ( X = 0 X=0 ).
Applying detailed binary stellar evolution modelling, Tauris et al. 2015 systematically investigated a wide parameter space
of initial orbital periods and helium-star masses to calculate the amount of mass accreted by the NS.
For binaries leading to formation of DNS systems they found: Δ ​ M NS = 5 × 10 − 5 − 3 × 10 − 3 ​ M ⊙ \Delta M_{\rm NS}=5\times 10^{-5}-3\times 10^{-3}\;M_{\odot} ,
where Δ ​ M NS \Delta M_{\rm NS} is systematically decreasing with increasing values of P orb P_{\rm orb} .
These values are in agreement with previous studies of Case BB RLO ( Dewi et al. 2002 ; Dewi & Pols 2003 ; Ivanova et al. 2003 ; Tauris et al. 2012 ; Lazarus et al. 2014 ) . 

Besides increasing the mass of the accreting NS star, Case BB RLO also serves to transfer
angular momentum and thus spin up this first-born NS to become observable as a (mildly) recycled radio pulsar.
Based on standard assumptions of the torque acting on the NS magnetosphere at the inner edge of the
accretion disk, combined with observational data of recycled pulsars in DNS systems,
 Tauris et al. 2012 and Lazarus et al. 2014 concluded that Case BB RLO should allow for NS accretion rates above the standard
value of M ˙ Edd \dot{M}_{\rm Edd} (see above) by a factor of at least 2 − 3 2-3 . This result seems to be
necessary to explain the fastest spins of recycled pulsars with NS or massive WD companions
(which are also believed to have evolved via post-CE Case BB RLO).
An X-ray binary can circumvent the value of M ˙ Edd \dot{M}_{\rm Edd} , for example, due to a strong B-field of the NS (see discussions in Section  V.2 ).
Hence, we conclude that Case BB RLO may, in a few cases, result in accretion of up to ( 6 − 9 ) × 10 − 3 ​ M ⊙ (6-9)\times 10^{-3}\;M_{\odot} . 

### IV.5. Shell impact from the SN of the secondary star

In case the secondary star explodes at a very close distance to the first-born NS, the latter
may, in principle, accrete material from the SN ejecta ( Fryer et al. 2014 ) .
However, in order for this to happen the evolution leading to the second SN event has to
be extremely finetuned with respect to both the preceding CE phase and the evolutionary stage
of the secondary star. The system would have to almost, but not quite, merge during the CE
and at the same time secure that the secondary star explodes before the system merges
as a result of GW radiation. Although one cannot rule out that such a finetuning might
be possible in a few extremely rare cases — leading to orbital periods of 2 − 10 ​ mins 2-10\;{\rm mins} at the
moment of the explosion — such a scenario can safely be ignored for DNS progenitors in general
and thus Δ ​ M NS ≪ 10 − 3 ​ M ⊙ \Delta M_{\rm NS}\ll 10^{-3}\;M_{\odot} . 

### IV.6. The NS accretion efficiency

Although the HMXB donor star (the progenitor of the second-born NS) provides the material for
potential accretion onto the first-born NS in all above-mentioned phases, it is not certain
how much of that material transfered will actually be accreted. For example, some of it may be lost
from the system due to ejector or propeller effects, accretion disk instabilities and direct irradiation
of the donor’s atmosphere from the pulsar ( Illarionov & Sunyaev 1975 ; van Paradijs 1996 ; Dubus et al. 1999 ; Romanova et al. 2009 , e.g.) .
Evidence of low NS accretion efficiencies in LMXB binaries is seen in several binary pulsar
systems with fully recycled MSPs and yet relative small NS masses ( Tauris & Savonije 1999 ; Antoniadis et al. 2012 ; Antoniadis et al. 2017 ) .
An extreme example is PSR J1918 − - 0642, which is an MSP with P = 7.6 ​ ms P=7.6\;{\rm ms} , in orbit with a low-mass He WD and P orb = 10.9 ​ days P_{\rm orb}=10.9\;{\rm days} ,
and yet it has a mass of only 1.18 − 0.09 + 0.10 ​ M ⊙ 1.18^{+0.10}_{-0.09}\;M_{\odot} ( Fonseca et al. 2016 ) . 

As discussed in Section  III.4 , only wide-orbit HMXBs are expected to survive CE evolution
and produce DNS systems ( Taam 1996 , e.g.) .
For such HMXBs, both Δ ​ M NS \Delta M_{\rm NS} and the efficiency of angular-momentum transfer is even more uncertain.
Moreover, there is evidence of quite a variety in NS accretion efficiencies from the location
of the different populations of HMXBs in the Corbet diagram ( Waters & van Kerkwijk 1989 ; Chaty 2013 ; Li et al. 2016 ) .
It is therefore likely that the estimated values of Δ ​ M NS \Delta M_{\rm NS} at various stages discussed above
may represent overestimates due to magnetospheric and accretion disk effects which were not taken into account. 

### IV.7. Total amount of mass gained by the first-born NS

Adding the expected amounts of material accreted by the first-born NS from each of the above-mentioned stages
leads to a total accumulated amount of Δ ​ M NS < 0.02 ​ M ⊙ \Delta M_{\rm NS}<0.02\;M_{\odot} . The exact amount depends on CE physics and
magnetospheric conditions, i.e. the B-field and the spin evolution of the NS.
The spin-up, on the other hand, is expected to be most efficient during Case BB RLO where an accretion disk
is always present ( Murguia-Berthier et al. 2017 , unlike the situation in a CE,) , leading to a strong and stable accretion torque acting on the NS.
Hence, the spin period of the recycled pulsar is primarily determined by the amount of accreted material during Case BB RLO. 

### IV.8. Spin periods of recycled NSs in DNS systems

The minimum amount of mass accreted to recycle a pulsar to reach a certain equilibrium
spin period is estimated to be roughly given by ( Tauris et al. 2012 , see discussions in Sections 4.3 and 4.4 in) 7 7 
 7 

 Under the assumption that the
location of the NS magnetosphere is approximately kept fixed and the accreting NS spins near/at equilibrium while accumulating most of its material. : 

 Δ ​ M eq ≃ 0.22 ​ M ⊙ ​ ( M NS / M ⊙ ) 1 / 3 P ms 4 / 3 . \Delta M_{\rm eq}\simeq 0.22\,M_{\odot}\;\frac{(M_{\rm NS}/M_{\odot})^{1/3}}{P_{\rm ms}^{4/3}}\,. 

 (5) 

From the fact that the observed spin periods of the (mildly) recycled NSs in DNS systems are in the interval
 23 − 185 ​ ms 23-185\;{\rm ms} (cf. Table  2 ), we notice that only a very modest amount,
 Δ ​ M NS = ( 0.2 − 4 ) × 10 − 3 ​ M ⊙ \Delta M_{\rm NS}=(0.2-4)\times 10^{-3}\;M_{\odot} is in principle needed to explain these spin periods.
Interestingly enough, these values of Δ ​ M NS \Delta M_{\rm NS} are in fine agreement with the
amount of accreted material derived above. 

According to the mass-transfer modelling of Tauris et al. 2015 , the fastest spinning recycled DNS pulsars (outside globular clusters) are expected to possess spin periods
down to ∼ 11 ​ ms \sim\!11\;{\rm ms} . Hence, no DNS systems are expected to be discovered with a spin period below this value.
This argument strengthens the hypothesis that the globular cluster DNS J1807 − - 2500B, which has a spin period of
only 4.2 ms, is the outcome of a dynamical encounter event ( Lynch et al. 2012 ) . This NS was most likely spun-up in a LMXB system and where the low-mass companion (WD)
was subsequently exchanged with a more massive ( 1.21 ​ M ⊙ 1.21\;M_{\odot} ) compact object to produce the presently observed DNS system. 

A caveat in the above discussion is the possibility of long-term accretion at a rate M ˙ NS ≫ M ˙ Edd \dot{M}_{\rm NS}\gg\dot{M}_{\rm Edd} . Such a high accretion rate is apparently evident in a
ULX system with a pulsating NS accretor ( Israel et al. 2017a ) . If such a ULX system is indeed a HMXB (rather than an IMXB, as we argue in favor of in Section  III.5 ),
and if such a system could avoid a delayed dynamical instability and actually form a DNS system, then it might be possible to produce a DNS system
in the Galactic disk with a NS being fully recycled to a spin period of 1 − 2 ​ ms 1-2\;{\rm ms} (or even a sub-ms pulsar). A remaining puzzle, however, is how the strong B-field (if this is
the reason behind a highly super-Eddington accreting NS) would remain strong without decaying ( Bhattacharya 2002 ) , given the vast amount of material accreted by such a NS.
If a hypothetical post-ULX DNS system hosted a recycled pulsar with a large B-field it would only be detectable for a short time due to rapid loss of rotational energy.
However, the second-born NS would be detectable as a normal pulsar. 

### IV.9. Birth masses of NSs in DNS systems

The above analysis demonstrates very limited accretion ( < 0.02 ​ M ⊙ <0.02\;M_{\odot} ) by the first-born NS during/after the HMXB phase, and
we can hereby conclude that the distribution of NS masses in DNS systems (Fig.  3 ), being either the first-born (mildly recycled) NS or the second-born (young) NS,
to a large degree reflects the birth distribution of NS masses in such observed systems.
Two interesting questions are therefore:
i) whether the mass difference (roughly ∼ 0.1 ​ M ⊙ \sim\!0.1\;M_{\odot} ) between the first- and the second-born NSs can be understood from a stellar evolution (and/or a SN explosion) point of view; and
ii) how the birth masses of the NSs in DNS systems compare to the measured masses of NSs observed in X-ray binaries or in binary radio pulsar systems with a WD companion. 

To answer the first question, it is of interest to consider
studies of the pre-SN stripping effects of the exploding stars in close orbits (Type Ib/c SNe). Due to the presence of a non-degenerate companion star in the first SN, it is clear that the progenitor
of the first-born NS ( Yoon et al. 2010 ) is usually stripped significantly less than the progenitor of the second-born NS ( Tauris et al. 2015 ) .
The reason being that in the second SN, the progenitor of the exploding star is being stripped significantly deeper when the companion is a NS.
This might give a natural explanation for potential differences in the resulting NS masses.
Note that we must be aware of potential selection effects.
If a small NS mass is connected with a small kick (see Section  VI.6 ), the systems with the smallest masses of the second-born NSs
have a larger probability of surviving the second SN. This provides a selection effect favouring low secondary NS masses to be overrepresented among the DNS systems.
In Section  VI.9 we demonstrate, however, that this selection effect is rather limited in practice. 

For the second question, we see in Fig.  3 that while the lower end of the NS mass distribution matches fairly well between
NSs in DNS and NS+WD systems, so far no massive NS ( ≥ 1.70 ​ M ⊙ \geq 1.70\;M_{\odot} ) has been found in a DNS system.
However, we are dealing with relatively small number statistics and we know of NS masses in HMXBs (the progenitors of DNS systems)
which may be as high as ∼ 2.0 ​ M ⊙ \sim\!2.0\;M_{\odot} , e.g. Vela X-1 ( Barziv et al. 2001 ; Falanga et al. 2015 ) .
Given that wide-orbit HMXBs are progenitors of DNS systems (Section  III.4 ), one would indeed expect the NS masses in these HMXBs to resemble the masses of
the first-born NSs in DNS systems based on our presented evidence for very limited accretion during/after the HMXB phase.
Unfortunately, the masses of NSs in HMXBs ( Falanga et al. 2015 ) are not measured to the same precision as radio pulsar masses, which limits the
possibility for a useful direct comparison. 

### V. The ( P orb , P , e ) (P_{\rm orb},\,P,\,e) –correlations

An important diagnostic tool for probing formation of DNS systems is an investigation of correlations between orbital parameters
(orbital period and eccentricity) and spin period of the recycled pulsars. Any such correlation will yield valuable information
about the previous mass-transfer phase and the SN explosion. The first such investigations were carried out by Dewi et al. 2002 ; Dewi & Pols 2003 ; Faulkner et al. 2005 ; Dewi et al. 2005 .
A similar method is applied when investigating the formation and evolution of Be-HMXBs (Sections  III.2 and III.4 )
following the first SN, see e.g. Pfahl et al. 2002 and Knigge et al. 2011 . 

In the following, we first demonstrate an empirical correlation between P orb P_{\rm orb} and P P (Section  V.1 )
followed by theoretical modelling of such a correlation (Section  V.2 ). We then discuss correlations between P orb P_{\rm orb} and
eccentricity (Section  V.3 ) and P P and eccentricity (Section  V.4 ), with a particular focus
on the NS kick from the second SN. More detailed analyses and discussions on NS kicks follow thereafter in Section  VI . 

### V.1. The ( P orb , P ) (P_{\rm orb},\,P) –correlation

In Fig.  6 , we have plotted the spin period of the recycled pulsars as a function of P orb P_{\rm orb} for all observed Galactic disk DNS systems.
A fit to the raw data results in the following correlation (grey line): 

 P = 44 ​ ms ​ ( P orb / days ) 0.26 , P=44\;{\rm ms}\;(P_{\rm orb}{\rm/days})^{0.26}\,, 

 (6) 

based on a linear regression in the ( log ⁡ P orb , log ⁡ P \log P_{\rm orb},\log P )–plane, and with a regression coefficient of R 2 = 0.73 R^{2}=0.73 .
However, as we shall demonstrate below, differences in the progenitor stars and their SN explosions result in a scatter in this diagram.
Furthermore, the observed data of DNS systems does not necessarily reflect their birth properties of P orb P_{\rm orb} and P P ,
since pulsars lose rotational energy with time, and for tight DNS systems the orbits decay due to GW radiation
(i.e. P P increases and P orb P_{\rm orb} decreases, cf. Section  VII ).
By taking these effects into account in a qualitative manner, and placing particular weight on the very wide-orbit DNS 1930 − - 1852 ( Swiggum et al. 2015 ) ,
we obtain the following simple empirical birth correlation (green line): 

 P ≈ 36 ± 14 ​ ms ​ ( P orb / days ) 0.40 ± 0.10 . P\approx 36\pm 14\;{\rm ms}\;(P_{\rm orb}{\rm/days})^{0.40\pm 0.10}. 

 (7) 

 Figure 6.— Spin period as a function of orbital period for all first-born (recycled) NSs in DNS systems.
The observational data are plotted with red points (the error bars are too small to be seen).
The grey line (Eq.  6 ) is a fit to the raw data.
The green line (Eq.  7 ) is our estimated empirical relation at birth (i.e. right after the second SN),
and the shaded region is its uncertainty.

A corresponding theoretical ( P orb , P ) (P_{\rm orb},\,P) –correlation can also be derived for DNS systems
and which can easily be understood qualitatively from binary stellar evolution arguments ( Tauris et al. 2015 ) .
The wider the initial orbit of the DNS progenitor system (i.e. the NS–helium star system following the CE phase), the more evolved is the
helium star by the time it fills its Roche lobe and initiates mass transfer. Hence, in a wide system the helium star has less time to transfer material toward the accreting NS
before it terminates its nuclear evolution and explodes in a SN. As a result, the wider the progenitor system is, the
less efficient is the recycling of the first-born NS and the larger (slower) is its rejuvenated spin period.
For example, helium star donors in wide-orbit systems may already be undergoing carbon shell burning at the onset of Case BB RLO (strictly speaking Case BC RLO)
whereby their remaining lifetime is less than a few thousand years before they collapse.
Such a case is demonstrated in Fig.  7 .
In the widest DNS systems, one would therefore expect to find NSs with P > 100 ​ ms P>100\;{\rm ms} and relatively large B-fields,
i.e. marginally recycled pulsars ( Tauris et al. 2015 ) .
The recently discovered DNS system PSR J1930 − - 1852 ( Swiggum et al. 2015 ) is an excellent example of such a case
( P = 185 ​ ms P=185\;{\rm ms} , P ˙ = 1.8 × 10 − 17 ​ s ​ s − 1 \dot{P}=1.8\times 10^{-17}\;{\rm s}\,{\rm s}^{-1} , P orb = 45 ​ days P_{\rm orb}=45\;{\rm days} , and eccentricity e = 0.40 e=0.40 , see Fig.  2 ).
Its discovery is important for testing formation scenarios of such wide-orbit marginally recycled pulsars. 

 Figure 7.— Kippenhahn diagram of a 3.0 ​ M ⊙ 3.0\;M_{\odot} helium star undergoing Case BB RLO to a NS
in a wide binary with P orb , i = 50 ​ days P_{\rm orb,i}=50\;{\rm days} (cf. data in Table  5 ).
The plot shows the evolving cross-section of the star, in mass coordinate on the y-axis, as a function of remaining
calculated lifetime on the x-axis. The green hatched areas denote zones with convection and the
intensity of the blue/purple colour indicates the net energy-production rate.
The total mass of the star is shown by the solid black line. For this wide-orbit binary
the onset of the RLO (marked by a red arrow) will occur at a late evolutionary stage (carbon shell burning)
such that the star will undergo core collapse within ∼ 1000 ​ yr \sim 1000\;{\rm yr} , i.e. before the end of the RLO.
Mass loss prior to RLO is due to a stellar wind. 

### V.2. Calculating a theoretical ( P orb , P ) (P_{\rm orb},\,P) –correlation

Based on a few assumptions, one can derive a theoretical ( P orb , P ) (P_{\rm orb},\,P) –correlation as follows.
From the detailed grid of Case BB RLO models by Tauris et al. 2015 we obtain the amount of material accreted by the NS, Δ ​ M NS \Delta M_{\rm NS} .
Assuming the NS reaches equilibrium spin during Case BB RLO, we can use Eq. ( 5 )
to estimate the spin period of the recycled pulsar, P P at the end of the mass-transfer phase ( Dewi et al. 2005 , see also) .
The orbital period at the end of Case BB RLO, just prior to the (ultra-stripped) SN explosion, is also provided by the models in Tauris et al. 2015 .
Assuming, as a first-order approximation, that this second SN is symmetric (without any kick imparted on the newborn NS, i.e. w = 0 w=0 , cf. Section  VI for discussions)
we can use simple analytic prescriptions to calculate the post-SN orbital period, P orb P_{\rm orb} and the eccentricity (cf. Section  VI.3 ). 

Fig.  8 shows examples of such theoretical ( P orb , P ) (P_{\rm orb},\,P) –correlations
based on purely symmetric SNe.
In this plot, we assumed in all cases an exploding star which is a stripped helium star with an
initial mass of 3.0 ​ M ⊙ 3.0\;M_{\odot} .
The parameters of the applied models from Tauris et al. 2015 are shown in Table  5 .
Three different blue curves are shown in Fig.  8 , depending on the assumed value for the Eddington accretion efficiency parameter, X Edd X_{\rm Edd} .
This parameter is a measure of the maximum NS accretion rate in units of the classical Eddington limit, X Edd ≡ M ˙ NS max / M ˙ Edd X_{\rm Edd}\equiv\dot{M}_{\rm NS}^{\rm max}/\dot{M}_{\rm Edd} .
The larger the value of X Edd X_{\rm Edd} , the more efficient is the recycling (more material is accreted by the NS),
and the smaller is the final spin period of the recycled pulsar.
The quoted values of Δ ​ M NS \Delta M_{\rm NS} in Tables  5 and 6 are based on an assumed accretion efficiency value of X Edd = 1 X_{\rm Edd}=1 .
However, as discussed in Section  IV.4 , it is possible that the amount of material accreted by the NS, Δ ​ M NS \Delta M_{\rm NS} is larger by
a factor of three, based on evidence for a high accretion efficiency in PSR J1952+2630 ( Lazarus et al. 2014 ) . 

Super-Eddington accretion by a factor of a few is easily obtained by a thick accretion disk with a central funnel ( Abramowicz et al. 1980 ) , or a
reduction in the electron scattering cross-section caused by a strong B-field ( Basko & Sunyaev 1976 ; Paczynski 1992 ) .
In fact, the latter effect might even be responsible for explaining the highly super-Eddington nature (factor ∼ 100 \sim\!100 ) of accreting
NSs in some ULXs, see Section  III.5 . However, as mentioned in Section  IV.8 , the current spin distribution
of observed DNS systems is fully consistent without the need for long-term accretion at rates exceeding M ˙ Edd \dot{M}_{\rm Edd} by a large factor. 

Applying a SN mass cut at the edge of the metal core, and using the release of gravitational binding energy following Lattimer & Yahil 1989 ,
give the mass of the newborn NS: M NS = M core , f − M NS bind M_{\rm NS}=M_{\rm core,f}-M_{\rm NS}^{\rm bind} , where the
released binding energy of the NS corresponds to a mass decrease of M NS bind = 0.084 ​ M ⊙ ⋅ ( M NS / M ⊙ ) 2 M_{\rm NS}^{\rm bind}=0.084\;M_{\odot}\cdot(M_{\rm NS}/M_{\odot})^{2} .
The resulting masses of the newborn NSs are in this specific case between 1.31 and 1.41 ​ M ⊙ 1.41\;M_{\odot} 
(cf. M NS , 2 M_{\rm NS,2} in Table  5 ), given the relatively small initial helium star masses, M He , i = 3.0 ​ M ⊙ M_{\rm He,i}=3.0\;M_{\odot} assumed prior to Case BB RLO.
Finally, knowing the mass of the newborn NS and all relevant pre-SN quantities, we are able to calculate the post-SN values of P orb P_{\rm orb} . 

 Figure 8.— Theoretical correlations between orbital period and spin period of the first-born NSs in DNS systems (blue lines),
calculated for three different values of the Eddington accretion efficiency parameter, X Edd X_{\rm Edd} ,
and assuming M He , i = 3.0 ​ M ⊙ M_{\rm He,i}=3.0\;M_{\odot} and no kick during the SN. The observational data are plotted with red points,
and the green line is our birth relation based on this data (cf. Fig.  6 ).

At first look from Fig.  8 , one could be tempted to conclude that theoretical modelling with X Edd ≃ 2.0 X_{\rm Edd}\simeq 2.0 can best reproduce
the observational data. However, as we shall now demonstrate, the location of each observed DNS system in any ( P orb , P , e ) (P_{\rm orb},\,P,\,e) –plane
is strongly dependent on both the mass of the progenitor of the exploding star (i.e. the helium star) and the magnitude and direction of the
kick during the second SN. 

 Figure 9.— Spread in the ( P orb , P ) (P_{\rm orb},\,P) –correlation is illustrated here (blue stars) when
applying a wide range of initial binary parameters ( M He , i M_{\rm He,i} and P orb , i P_{\rm orb,i} )
which lead to quite different masses and orbital periods of the exploding helium stars.
Calculations are based on data in Table  6 and assuming a purely symmetric SN ( w = 0 w=0 ).
The observational data are plotted with red points,
and the green line is our birth relation based on this data (cf. Fig.  6 ). 

To illustrate the spread in theoretically estimated points in the ( P orb , P ) (P_{\rm orb},\,P) –plane using different initial masses of the
progenitor of the exploding star, we show in Fig.  9 a number of points based on sample models from Tauris et al. 2015 for different
initial helium star masses in the interval M He , i = 2.6 − 6.0 ​ M ⊙ M_{\rm He,i}=2.6-6.0\;M_{\odot} and with different initial orbital periods (cf. Table  6 ).
In these models, the masses of the helium stars at the moment of the (electron capture or iron core collapse, see Section  VI ) SN explosions are 1.43 − 2.37 ​ M ⊙ 1.43-2.37\;M_{\odot} ,
with metal cores of 1.37 − 2.15 ​ M ⊙ 1.37-2.15\;M_{\odot} .
The resulting NS masses cover a range between 1.2 − 1.9 ​ M ⊙ 1.2-1.9\;M_{\odot} . This range is possibly wider by ± 0.1 ​ M ⊙ \pm 0.1\;M_{\odot} ,
if accounting for the uncertainty in the location of the mass cut and the NS EoS.
For the theoretical values, shown as blue stars in Fig.  9 , we assumed a fixed value of X Edd = 2.0 X_{\rm Edd}=2.0 .
The fact that the scatter of observed data points is smaller than that obtained from our applied range of theoretical models
suggests some uniformity of the parent (pre-SN) systems.
Note, following the method described above, we also find evidence for the formation of a few DNS systems outside the range of the plane shown in Fig.  9 ,
i.e. DNS systems with larger values of either P orb P_{\rm orb} or P P . 

The presence of (even small) kicks also contributes significantly to smearing out the expected theoretical correlation
between P orb P_{\rm orb} and P P obtained for symmetric SNe. In the following sections we illustrate the effect of an asymmetric SN.

### V.3. The ( P orb , e ) (P_{\rm orb},\,e) –correlation

In Fig.  10 , we have plotted the eccentricities of observed DNS systems (red points)
as a function of their orbital periods. The measured error bars are too small to be seen. Also, a (weak) correlation between P orb P_{\rm orb} and eccentricity
is expected from binary stellar evolution during Case BB RLO ( Tauris et al. 2015 ) .
Again, the argument relates to the decreasing amount of material transfered from
helium star donors with increasing values of P orb P_{\rm orb} because of their shorter lifetimes prior to collapse
— see fig. 16 in Tauris et al. 2015 .
As a result, these wide-orbit stars possess a large envelope mass by the time they explode which increases
the instantaneous mass loss during the SN, thereby increasing the post-SN eccentricity (see dashed blue line in Fig.  10 
as an illustrative example for symmetric SNe without a kick).
However, this correlation also depends on the initial values of M He , i M_{\rm He,i} .
Furthermore, it is more sensitive to even small kicks compared to the ( P orb , P ) (P_{\rm orb},\,P) –correlation. 

 Figure 10.— Eccentricity as a function of orbital period for all confirmed DNS systems observed in the Galactic disk (red points).
Black dots are simulated systems based on
models from Tauris et al. 2015 , using M He , i = 3.0 ​ M ⊙ M_{\rm He,i}=3.0\;M_{\odot} and P orb , i = 0.08 − 100 ​ days P_{\rm orb,i}=0.08-100\;{\rm days} .
For each model, 1000 resulting bound DNS systems were simulated based on SNe with a fixed kick of w = 50 ​ km ​ s − 1 w=50\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} 
and applied in a random (isotropic) direction. Unbound systems are not included. The dashed blue line
is connecting discrete data point solutions for the resulting correlation in case of symmetric SNe ( w = 0 w=0 ). 

Indeed, the observed spread in eccentricities for a given value of P orb P_{\rm orb} can be understood if the second SN
in these binaries (producing the second-born, and non-recycled, pulsar) is even slightly asymmetric, e.g. w ≲ 50 ​ km ​ s − 1 w\lesssim 50\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} .
In Fig.  10 , we simulated the dynamical effects of applying kicks of w = 50 ​ km ​ s − 1 w=50\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} to the stellar
models presented in Table  5 with circular orbits prior to the SN. We performed 1000 trials with randomly oriented (isotropic) kicks.
As can be seen from the figure, the resulting post-SN systems are spread out over a large area in the ( P orb , e ) (P_{\rm orb},\,e) – plane.
An immediate result, however, is that with such small kicks we cannot explain two of the DNS systems.
These are the well-known PSR B1913+16 (the Hulse-Taylor pulsar, P orb = 0.323 ​ days P_{\rm orb}=0.323\;{\rm days} and e = 0.617 e=0.617 ) and
PSR J1811 − - 1736 ( P orb = 18.8 ​ days P_{\rm orb}=18.8\;{\rm days} and e = 0.828 e=0.828 ). As we shall discuss in more detail in Section  VIII ,
the latter DNS system can actually be reproduced from a small kick by increasing the value of M He , i M_{\rm He,i} ,
whereas PSR B1913+16 must have had w ≳ 200 ​ km ​ s − 1 w\gtrsim 200\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} for any mass of the exploding star to match observations. 

In relatively wide-orbit pre-SN binaries, even small kicks are able to produce a full range of post-SN eccentricities
between 0 < e < 1 0<e<1 , or even disrupt the system. This is demonstrated in Fig.  11 , where we modelled the
explosions of M He , f = 3.0 ​ M ⊙ M_{\rm He,f}=3.0\;M_{\odot} stars with a fixed kick magnitude of w = 50 ​ km ​ s − 1 w=50\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} , and varied the direction
of the applied kicks by systematically changing the two kick angles, θ \theta and ϕ \phi (see Section  VI for a definition).
The pre-SN orbital period was assumed to be P orb , f = 25 ​ days P_{\rm orb,f}=25\;{\rm days} in all cases.
It is seen that systems with θ \theta less than some critical value, θ crit ≃ 83 ∘ \theta_{\rm crit}\simeq 83^{\circ} will always be disrupted
— cf. discussions in Section  VI . Another version of this plot, for an assumed ultra-stripped SN with a large kick
in a tight orbit, is shown in the Appendix (Fig.  40 ). 

 Figure 11.— Dependence of the post-SN eccentricity on the direction of the kick (see Fig.  13 ).
About half (56%) of the systems remain bound, with an average 3D systemic velocity of ⟨ v sys ⟩ = 40 ​ km ​ s − 1 \langle v_{\rm sys}\rangle=40\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} .
The value of their eccentricities ( 0 < e < 1 0<e<1 ) are color coded, cf. scale.
All systems with θ < θ crit \theta<\theta_{\rm crit} , however, will not survive the kick.
The small black areas in centre of the red regions mark almost circular post-SN systems with e < 0.015 e<0.015 .
The three arrows on the left side mark (bottom to top): θ crit \theta_{\rm crit} , θ a \theta_{\rm a} and θ P \theta_{\rm P} — see Section  VI .
For another version of this plot, see Fig.  40 . 

### V.4. The ( P , e ) (P,\,e) –correlation

In the previous sections, we have investigated the correlations for DNS systems between P orb P_{\rm orb} and P P , and between P orb P_{\rm orb} and eccentricity,
based on binary stellar evolution calculations starting from NS–helium star binaries. As a consequence, one might also expect a correlation between P P and eccentricity.
Indeed, such a correlation for DNS systems was suggested by McLaughlin et al. 2005 ; Faulkner et al. 2005 , and investigated theoretically by Dewi et al. 2005 , based on the discovery
of the first seven DNS systems which could be fitted reasonably well onto a straight line in the ( P , e ) (P,\,e) –plane. 

 Faulkner et al. 2005 suggested that in NS–helium star binaries, where a large amount of material
is transfered from the helium star to the NS, leading to efficient spin up and thus a small value of P P ,
less material will be ejected during the SN explosion which results in smaller eccentricities.
This qualitative argument is in good agreement with the more detailed analysis presented here and in Tauris et al. 2015 . 

In Fig.  12 , we show such an expected correlation between P P and eccentricity for the same systems
displayed in Fig.  10 (cf. Table  5 ),
and which subsequently exploded in either symmetric SNe ( w = 0 w=0 , dashed blue line) or in a SNe with a fixed kick of w = 50 ​ km ​ s − 1 w=50\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} .
The solid blue line represents the outcome of our theoretical models with applied kicks of w = 50 ​ km ​ s − 1 w=50\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} averaged over all kick directions
which resulted in bound systems.
We assumed again an Eddington accretion efficiency parameter X Edd = 2.0 X_{\rm Edd}=2.0 for the NS.
It is clear that the observed systems (red points) do not follow the theoretical curve for a symmetric SN ( w = 0 w=0 ).
The reason is again, that even a small asymmetry in the SNe will affect the eccentricities ( Dewi et al. 2005 , see also simulations and discussion in) .
This is also seen in Fig.  12 , where the black dots clustered on discrete vertical lines represent MC simulations
for each of the 10 values of P orb , i P_{\rm orb,i} , assuming a random (isotropic) kick direction for a fixed kick magnitude of w = 50 ​ km ​ s − 1 w=50\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} (see also Fig.  10 ).
Note, considering only the eccentricities of the DNS systems as a function of P P we can explain (almost) all of
the observed systems using w ≤ 50 ​ km ​ s − 1 w\leq 50\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} . However, there are further observational constraints than
 P P , P orb P_{\rm orb} and eccentricity, such as proper motion, which in some cases require larger values of w w (see discussions in Sections  VI and VIII ). 

In the previous four subsections (Sections  V.1 – V.4 ), we have discussed the correlations
between P P , P orb P_{\rm orb} and eccentricity for DNS systems, as expected from theoretical modelling of binary pre-SN stars
and mainly under the assumption of purely symmetric SNe. However, we have also demonstrated (cf. Figs.  9 , 10 and
 12 ) that applying different masses of the exploding star and/or taking into account (even small) kicks will camouflage
any such correlation. We now continue discussing kicks in the context of a ( P , e ) (P,\,e) –correlation. 

 Figure 12.— Eccentricity as a function of spin period for all first-born (recycled) NSs in DNS systems.
The observational data are shown with red dots. The error bars are too small to be seen.
Black dots along discrete vertical lines are simulated systems based on models from Tauris et al. 2015 , see Fig.  10 for details.
The dashed and solid blue lines show the resulting correlation for w = 0 w=0 and the average values of eccentricity for bound systems using w = 50 ​ km ​ s − 1 w=50\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} , respectively. 

### V.4.1 Kick magnitudes and selection effects

The importance of the kick magnitude during the second SN in DNS systems for the suggested correlation between P P and eccentricity
was investigated in detail by Dewi et al. 2005 using a population synthesis method, also taking into account orbital and spin evolution
since the birth of a given DNS system. They found that large kicks (using a Maxwellian kick velocity distribution with a 1D dispersion of σ = 190 ​ km ​ s − 1 \sigma=190\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} )
would completely randomize any correlation, whereas small kicks with typically w < 50 ​ km ​ s − 1 w<50\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} ( σ = 20 ​ km ​ s − 1 \sigma=20\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} ) preserve a
correlation. 

In addition, Dewi et al. 2005 found that the ( P , e ) (P,\,e) –correlation cannot be explained simply by selection effects. Such a hypothesis
had been suggested by Chaurasia & Bailes 2005 , who argued that the correlation is, at least partly, caused by close-orbit (thus small P P ) systems with high eccentricities
merging on a short timescale due to GWs, thereby removing them from the observed sample.
This hypothesis would then explain an observed DNS population with some correlation between
 P P and eccentricity. However, using population synthesis, Dewi et al. 2005 showed that although close-orbit DNS systems are removed
from the expected observed sample due to merger events, the remaining systems still show a random distribution of eccentricities for
a given value of P P if the applied kicks are large ( σ = 190 ​ km ​ s − 1 \sigma=190\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} , corresponding to average kicks of ⟨ w ⟩ = 450 ​ km ​ s − 1 \langle w\rangle=450\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} ). Only by applying a small kick distribution
( σ = 20 ​ km ​ s − 1 \sigma=20\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} , ⟨ w ⟩ = 47 ​ km ​ s − 1 \langle w\rangle=47\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} ), does a correlation between P P and eccentricity remain — in agreement with our findings.
As we shall discuss further below, a small kick imparted onto the newborn NS in the second SN is indeed, in general, providing a better fit to observations ( Bray & Eldridge 2016 , see also) .
Further discussions on selection effects of the observed population due to kicks are discussed in Section  VI.9 . 

### VI. General dynamical effects of SN e 

The dynamical effects of SNe in close binaries (and hierarchical triples) have been studied both analytically and numerically in a number of papers
over the last four decades, since the discovery of the Hulse-Taylor pulsar,
e.g. Flannery & van den Heuvel 1975 ; Sutantyo 1978 ; Hills 1983 ; Brandt & Podsiadlowski 1995 ; Tauris & Bailes 1996 ; Kalogera 1996 ; Tauris & Takens 1998 ; Wex et al. 2000 ; Pijloo et al. 2012 . 

To solve for the dynamical effects of the SN explosion, the SN event can be assumed to be instantaneous given that
the SN ejecta velocity is much greater than the binary orbital velocity 8 8 
 8 

 We ignore the extremely rare
cases discussed in Section  IV.5 . .
The mass loss reduces the absolute value of the potential energy and affects the orbital kinetic energy
by decreasing the reduced mass of the system. In addition, a kick is imparted on the newborn NS. Considering the change in
total energy of the system, the change in the orbital semi-major axis (ratio of final to initial value)
can be expressed by ( Hills 1983 ) : 

 a f a i = [ 1 − Δ ​ M / M 1 − 2 ​ Δ ​ M / M − ( w / v rel ) 2 − 2 ​ cos ⁡ θ ⁡ ( w / v rel ) ] , \frac{a_{\rm f}}{a_{\rm i}}=\left[\frac{1-\Delta M/M}{1-2\Delta M/M-(w/v_{\rm rel})^{2}-2\cos\theta\,(w/v_{\rm rel})}\right]\;, 

 (8) 

where Δ ​ M \Delta M is the amount of instantaneous mass loss from the exploding star (in our notation for the second SNe applied here,
 Δ ​ M = M He , f − M NS , 2 \Delta M=M_{\rm He,f}-M_{\rm NS,2} ), M = M He , f + M NS , 1 M=M_{\rm He,f}+M_{\rm NS,1} is the total mass of the pre-SN system and
 v rel v_{\rm rel} is the relative velocity between the two stars ( G ⁡ ( M He , f + M NS , 1 ) / a i \sqrt{G(M_{\rm He,f}+M_{\rm NS,1})/a_{\rm i}} ).
The kick angle, θ \theta is defined as the angle between the kick velocity vector, w → \vec{w} and the
pre-SN orbital velocity vector of the exploding star, v → He \vec{v}_{\rm He} in the pre-SN centre-of-mass rest frame (cf. Fig.  13 ).
Using Kepler’s third law, the change in P orb P_{\rm orb} can be obtained. 

Eq. ( 8 ) applies to a circular pre-SN binary. This is most likely a good approximation here given the tidal interactions during Case BB RLO.
For the more general case, see Hills 1983 .
While the shell-impact effects on the companion star from exploding helium stars in tight binaries can potentially
be important for the first SN explosion ( Wheeler et al. 1975 ; Tauris 2015 ; Liu et al. 2015 , e.g.) , we disregard such effects in the second SN where the companion star is a NS
(essentially a point mass). 

Solving for the denominator being equal to zero in Eq. ( 8 ) yields the critical angle, θ crit \theta_{\rm crit} ,
so that θ < θ crit \theta<\theta_{\rm crit} will result in the disruption of the orbit, cf. Fig.  11 . Thus, the probability of a binary system
surviving a SN with a kick in a random (isotropic) orientation can be found by integration and yields
 P bound = 1 − ( 1 − cos ⁡ θ crit ) / 2 P_{\rm bound}=1-(1-\cos\theta_{\rm crit})/2 , or equivalently ( Sutantyo 1978 ; Hills 1983 ) : 

 P bound = 1 2 ​ { 1 + [ 1 − 2 ​ Δ ​ M / M − ( w / v rel ) 2 2 ​ ( w / v rel ) ] } , P_{\rm bound}=\frac{1}{2}\,\bigg\{1+\left[\frac{1-2\Delta M/M-(w/v_{\rm rel})^{2}}{2\,(w/v_{\rm rel})}\right]\bigg\}\;, 

 (9) 

where P bound P_{\rm bound} is restricted to [0, 1], and takes the value of 0 or 1, below and above this interval, respectively. 

The eccentricity of the post-SN system can be evaluated directly from the post-SN orbital angular momentum, L orb , f L_{\rm orb,f} and is given by: 

 e = 1 + 2 ​ E orb , f ​ L orb , f 2 μ f ​ G 2 ​ M NS , 1 2 ​ M NS , 2 2 , e=\sqrt{1+\frac{2\,E_{\rm orb,f}\,L_{\rm orb,f}^{2}}{\mu_{\rm f}\,G^{2}M_{\rm NS,1}^{2}M_{\rm NS,2}^{2}}}\;, 

 (10) 

where 

 L orb , f = a i ​ μ f ​ ( v rel + w ​ cos ⁡ θ ) 2 + ( w ​ sin ⁡ θ ​ sin ⁡ ϕ ) 2 . L_{\rm orb,f}=a_{\rm i}\,\mu_{\rm f}\,\sqrt{\left(v_{\rm rel}+w\cos\theta\right)^{2}+\left(w\sin\theta\sin\phi\right)^{2}}\;. 

 (11) 

Here μ f \mu_{\rm f} and E orb , f = − G M NS , 1 M NS , 2 / 2 a f E_{\rm orb,f}=-GM_{\rm NS,1}M_{\rm NS,2}/2a_{\rm f} are the post-SN reduced mass and orbital energy, respectively.
The kick angle, ϕ \phi is measured in the plane perpendicular to the pre-SN velocity vector of the exploding star, v → He \vec{v}_{\rm He} ( Hills 1983 ; Tauris & Takens 1998 ) ,
such that the component of the kick velocity pointing directly toward to companion star is given by w y = w ​ sin ⁡ θ ​ cos ⁡ ϕ w_{y}=w\,\sin\theta\,\cos\phi (Fig.  13 ). 

 Figure 13.— Illustration of the geometry of the two kick angles ( θ , ϕ \theta,\phi ) and the kick sphere surrounding the exploding star (blue).
The pre-SN orbital angular momentum vector is along the z-axis.
The kick velocity vector ( w → \vec{w} ) is shown in red. 

Solving for the right-hand side in Eq. ( 8 ) being equal to 1 yields another interesting angle, θ a \theta_{\rm a} : 

 θ a = cos − 1 ⁡ ( − Δ ​ M / M + ( w / v rel ) 2 2 ​ ( w / v rel ) ) , \theta_{\rm a}=\cos^{-1}\,\left(-\,\frac{\Delta M/M+(w/v_{\rm rel})^{2}}{2\,(w/v_{\rm rel})}\right)\;, 

 (12) 

which determines whether the post-SN semi-major axis, a f a_{\rm f} is larger or smaller than the pre-SN orbital radius, a i a_{\rm i} .
Thus, as a consequence of the SN explosion the orbit widens 9 9 
 9 

 It should be noted that in case the term in the parenthesis in Eq. ( 12 ) takes a value less than − 1 -1 , it follows that
 θ a = 180 ∘ \theta_{\rm a}=180^{\circ} , meaning that all post-SN systems will widen. if the kick angle θ < θ a \theta<\theta_{\rm a} and it shrinks if θ > θ a \theta>\theta_{\rm a} .
The ratio ( a f / a i a_{\rm f}/a_{\rm i} ) is a simple monotonically decreasing function of θ \theta (cf. Eq.  8 ) such that minimum
size post-SN orbits are achieved for θ → 180 ∘ \theta\rightarrow 180^{\circ} .
For random kick directions, we find that the probability that the post-SN orbit shrinks ( a f < a i a_{\rm f}<a_{\rm i} ) is given by 10 10 
 10 

 P a − P^{-}_{\rm a} is restricted to [0,  1 2 \frac{1}{2} ]
and is always 0 below this interval. : 

 P a − = 1 2 − [ Δ ​ M / M + ( w / v rel ) 2 4 ​ ( w / v rel ) ] . P^{-}_{\rm a}=\frac{1}{2}-\left[\frac{\Delta M/M+(w/v_{\rm rel})^{2}}{4\,(w/v_{\rm rel})}\right]\;. 

 (13) 

This expression is convenient to use as an indicator for estimating the distribution of merger timescales of DNS systems produced by the second SN. 

Combining Eqs. ( 10 ) and ( 12 ) we can thus derive the two finetuned solutions for the kick angles
for which the post-SN system is circular (i.e. e = 0 e=0 ). The result is simply: ( θ , ϕ ) = ( θ a , ± 90 ∘ ) (\theta,\,\phi)=(\theta_{\rm a},\,\pm 90^{\circ}) ,
see Fig.  11 for an example. 

As a final twist in the tail, using Kepler’s third law we can also work out a critical angle, θ P \theta_{\rm P} : 

 θ P = cos − 1 ⁡ ( 1 − 2 ​ Δ ​ M / M − ( 1 − Δ ​ M / M ) 2 / 3 − ( w / v rel ) 2 2 ​ ( w / v rel ) ) , \theta_{\rm P}=\cos^{-1}\,\left(\frac{1-2\Delta M/M-(1-\Delta M/M)^{2/3}-(w/v_{\rm rel})^{2}}{2\,(w/v_{\rm rel})}\right)\;, 

 (14) 

above which the post-SN orbital period is smaller than the pre-SN orbital period. The probability that θ > θ P \theta>\theta_{\rm P} (and therefore that P orb P_{\rm orb} decreases)
is thus given by: 

 P P − = 1 2 − [ 2 ​ Δ ​ M / M + ( 1 − Δ ​ M / M ) 2 / 3 + ( w / v rel ) 2 − 1 4 ​ ( w / v rel ) ] . P^{-}_{\rm P}=\frac{1}{2}-\left[\frac{2\Delta M/M+(1-\Delta M/M)^{2/3}+(w/v_{\rm rel})^{2}-1}{4\,(w/v_{\rm rel})}\right]\;. 

 (15) 

It is always the case that θ crit < θ a < θ P \theta_{\rm crit}<\theta_{\rm a}<\theta_{\rm P} for any value of w w and Δ ​ M > 0 \Delta M>0 .
Thus, for systems with a kick angle θ a < θ < θ P \theta_{\rm a}<\theta<\theta_{\rm P} , the orbital semi-major axis shrinks while at the same time
the orbital period increases as a result of the SN. Examples of θ crit \theta_{\rm crit} , θ a \theta_{\rm a} and θ P \theta_{\rm P} 
for a given SN explosion are shown in Fig.  11 . Finally, constraints on retrograde versus prograde post-SN spin–orbits are discussed
in Hills 1983 ; Brandt & Podsiadlowski 1995 . 

### VI.1. Misalignment angles

If the kick applied to the second-born NS is directed out of the orbital plane of the pre-SN system (i.e. if the kick angle
 ϕ ≠ 0 ∘ \phi\neq 0^{\circ} and ϕ ≠ ± 180 ∘ \phi\neq\pm 180^{\circ} ), then the spin axis of the recycled pulsar will be tilted with respect to
the post-SN orbital angular momentum vector. This misalignment angle can be calculated as ( Hills 1983 ) : 

 δ = tan − 1 ⁡ ( w ​ sin ⁡ θ ​ sin ⁡ ϕ ( v rel + w ​ cos ⁡ θ ) 2 + ( w ​ sin ⁡ θ ​ cos ⁡ ϕ ) 2 ) \delta=\tan^{-1}\left(\frac{w\sin\theta\sin\phi}{\sqrt{(v_{\rm rel}+w\cos\theta)^{2}+(w\sin\theta\cos\phi)^{2}}}\right) 

 (16) 

and leads to geodetic precession of the recycled pulsar ( Kramer 1998 , e.g.) .
To use the observed misalignment angle to constrain kick properties, we must rely on the assumption that accretion torques align
the spin axis of the first-born NS with the orbital angular momentum vector during the recycling process ( Hills 1983 ; Bhattacharya & van den Heuvel 1991 , e.g.) .
The fact that no observable effects of geodetic precession have been measured for pulsar A in the double pulsar system J0737 − - 3039, implies that
 δ < 3.2 ∘ \delta<3.2^{\circ} ( Ferdman et al. 2013 ) and thus supports our assumption that δ i = 0 \delta_{i}=0 prior to the second SN explosion in all DNS systems.
Further observational evidence that such a spin-orbit alignment actually occurs during accretion in general was demonstrated for LMXBs by Guillemot & Tauris 2014 ,
who found agreement between the viewing angles of binary MSPs (as inferred from γ \gamma -ray light-curve modelling)
and their orbital inclination angles. Although the timescale of accretion during Case BB RLO in DNS progenitor systems is substantially
shorter (by two to four orders of magnitude) than that in LMXBs, the torques at work will be larger due to the
much higher mass-transfer rates during Case BB RLO (while the size of the magnetospheres remain roughly the same as a result of
larger NS B-fields in DNS systems compared to fully recycled MSPs). Therefore, we find that it is reasonable to assume δ i = 0 \delta_{i}=0 prior to
the second SN explosion and thus legitimate to use the post-SN measurements of δ \delta of the recycled NSs to constrain kicks
in the second SN event. 

### VI.2. Systemic velocities

Another important diagnostic quantity (besides from P orb P_{\rm orb} , P P , eccentricity, M NS M_{\rm NS} and δ \delta ) for understanding DNS formation is
their post-SN systemic velocity, v sys v_{\rm sys} .
Any DNS system receives a recoil velocity relative to the centre-of-mass rest frame of the pre-SN system.
This is due to the combined effects of sudden mass loss and a kick velocity imparted on the newborn NS.
From simple conservation of momentum considerations ( Tauris & Bailes 1996 , e.g. following) we can write this 3D velocity as: 

 v sys = ( Δ ​ p x ) 2 + ( Δ ​ p y ) 2 + ( Δ ​ p z ) 2 / ( M NS , 1 + M NS , 2 ) , v_{\rm sys}=\sqrt{(\Delta p_{x})^{2}+(\Delta p_{y})^{2}+(\Delta p_{z})^{2}}\;/\;(M_{\rm NS,1}+M_{\rm NS,2})\;, 

 (17) 

where the change in momentum is given by: 

 Δ ​ p x = M NS , 2 ​ w ​ cos ⁡ θ − Δ ​ M ​ M NS , 1 ​ G / ( M ​ a i ) , Δ ​ p y = M NS , 2 ​ w ​ sin ⁡ θ ​ cos ⁡ ϕ , Δ ​ p z = M NS , 2 ​ w ​ sin ⁡ θ ​ sin ⁡ ϕ . \begin{array}[]{lll}\vskip 5.69046pt\quad\Delta p_{x}=M_{\rm NS,2}\,w\cos\theta-\Delta MM_{\rm NS,1}\sqrt{G/(Ma_{\rm i})}\;,\\
\vskip 5.69046pt\quad\Delta p_{y}=M_{\rm NS,2}\,w\sin\theta\cos\phi\;,\\
\quad\Delta p_{z}=M_{\rm NS,2}\,w\sin\theta\sin\phi\;.\\
\end{array} 

 (18) 

As an example, Fig.  14 shows the calculated 3D systemic velocities from the simulations shown in Fig.  10 
where randomly oriented kicks with w = 50 ​ km ​ s − 1 w=50\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} were applied.
For these resulting systems, the average systemic velocity is ⟨ v sys ⟩ ≃ 30 ​ km ​ s − 1 \langle v_{\rm sys}\rangle\simeq 30\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} , which is still larger than
the values observed for some of the DNS systems (Table  3 ) and thus indicating again that, at least in some
cases, the kicks during the second SN have magnitudes of w < 50 ​ km ​ s − 1 w<50\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} 
(see detailed discussions further below). 

Finally, in addition to the kinematic effects from the second SN, DNS systems also have a relic systemic velocity component from
the first SN. This value, however, is expected to be small. Based on
the distribution and the peculiar velocities of HMXBs, Coleiro & Chaty 2013 find that their resulting systemic velocities
are often only ∼ 10 − 20 ​ km ​ s − 1 \sim\!10-20\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} .
Even after taking into account the momentum absorbed by the massive companion star in HMXBs we find that this result
supports the hypothesis that exploding stars generally produce smaller kicks compared to the distribution of Hobbs et al. 2005 , if they have lost (at least) their
hydrogen-rich envelope via mass transfer prior to the SN (see Section  VI.4 for discussions). 

 Figure 14.— Distribution of v sys v_{\rm sys} for the simulated DNS systems shown in Fig.  10 (using w = 50 ​ km ​ s − 1 w=50\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} );
see text for discussions. 

### VI.3. Symmetric SNe

For purely symmetric SNe ( w = 0 w=0 ), the equations governing the dynamical effects of the SN explosion (Eqs.  8 – 10 ) simplify to ( Flannery & van den Heuvel 1975 ) : 

 a f a i = [ 1 − Δ ​ M / M 1 − 2 ​ Δ ​ M / M ] , \frac{a_{\rm f}}{a_{\rm i}}=\left[\frac{1-\Delta M/M}{1-2\Delta M/M}\right]\,, 

 (19) 

and 

 e = Δ ​ M / ( M NS , 1 + M NS , 2 ) , e=\Delta M/(M_{\rm NS,1}+M_{\rm NS,2})\,, 

 (20) 

and where the probability of remaining bound is always P bound = 1 P_{\rm bound}=1 for Δ ​ M / M < 0.5 \Delta M/M<0.5 , whereas
(following the virial theorem) all systems are disrupted if more than half of the total mass is lost, i.e. if Δ ​ M / M > 0.5 \Delta M/M>0.5 . 

### VI.3.1 The eccentricity floor and the NS EoS

If the second SN in DNS systems were always symmetric ( w = 0 w=0 ) and the amount of SN ejecta mass negligible
(i.e. assuming extremely ultra-stripped exploding cores), then the only contribution to Δ ​ M \Delta M would be the released
gravitational binding energy ( M NS bind M_{\rm NS}^{\rm bind} ). This is released when baryonic mass is converted
into gravitational mass during the collapse (see e.g. Section  V.2 ). Under such circumstances
one would expect to observe an “eccentricity floor” at approximately e = Δ ​ M / M ≃ 0.06 e=\Delta M/M\simeq 0.06 .
From observations of many DNS systems, the exact inferred value of this “eccentricity floor” would
provide a measure of the released gravitational binding energy, E bind E_{\rm bind} and thereby constrain the NS EoS ( Lazarus et al. 2016 ) . 

Unfortunately, this method is difficult in practice since even ultra-stripped NS progenitors have small amounts ( < 0.20 ​ M ⊙ <0.20\;M_{\odot} ) of baryonic SN ejecta mass.
For symmetric explosions with such small amounts of ejecta mass, the post-SN eccentricities of DNS systems are expected to be e ≃ 0.07 − 0.13 e\simeq 0.07-0.13 ,
in agreement with constraints obtained from some of the known DNS systems (cf. Section  VIII ).
Such low eccentricities are, however, also possible for asymmetric SNe, depending on the kick direction.
Furthermore, even small kicks of w ∼ 50 ​ km ​ s − 1 w\sim 50\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} will generally lead to a substantial spread in eccentricities (also to cases with e < 0.06 e<0.06 ),
as we have demonstrated here in Fig.  10 ,
thereby making the idea of constraining the NS EoS impossible in practice using this method.
Finally, in sufficiently tight binaries gravitational damping may have decreased the values of the eccentricity
significantly since the second SN explosion ( Lazarus et al. 2016 ) . 

### VI.4. NS kick magnitudes in binaries

Whereas average kicks of 400 − 500 ​ km ​ s − 1 400-500\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} have been demonstrated for young isolated radio pulsars ( Lyne & Lorimer 1994 ; Hobbs et al. 2005 ) ,
it has been suggested for a couple of decades that exploding stars which are stripped in close binaries (i.e. Type Ib/c SNe) may produce substantially
smaller NS kicks compared to Type II SN explosions of isolated, or very wide-orbit, stars ( Tauris & Bailes 1996 ; Pfahl et al. 2002 ; Voss & Tauris 2003 ; Podsiadlowski et al. 2004 ; van den Heuvel 2004 ; Dewi et al. 2005 ; Bray & Eldridge 2016 ) .
This awareness came about from both theoretical and observational arguments —
the former along the lines of stripped stars often leading to relatively fast explosions with small kicks (see Section  VI.5 ), and the latter
from comparison of the observed radio pulsar velocity distribution, or population synthesis studies based on this distribution,
with the space velocities, orbital periods and eccentricities of both X-ray binaries, MSPs and DNS systems.
It is simply not possible to reproduce the observed data if exploding stars in close binaries, in general, would receive kicks of 400 − 500 ​ km ​ s − 1 400-500\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} .
The conclusion also holds when selection effects are taken into account (Section  VI.9 ). 

Indeed, the evidence for small kicks has already been pointed out in several studies ( Portegies Zwart & Yungelson 1998 ; Pfahl et al. 2002 ; Podsiadlowski et al. 2004 ; van den Heuvel 2004 ; Piran & Shaviv 2005 ; Schwab et al. 2010 ; Ferdman et al. 2013 ; Ferdman et al. 2014 ; Beniamini & Piran 2016 , e.g.) .
Moreover, for PSR J0737 − - 3039 (the double pulsar) and PSR J1756 − - 2251, Ferdman et al. 2013 ; Ferdman et al. 2014 derived small
misalignment angles from observations of these systems, giving further support for small kicks. 

On the other hand, we have clear evidence that even relatively large kicks can happen in close-orbit DNS progenitor systems.
For example, to explain the characteristics (i.e. large proper motions) of the Hulse-Taylor pulsar (PSR B1913+16) and PSR B1534+12, a kick of
at least w ≃ 200 ​ km ​ s − 1 w\simeq 200\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} is needed ( Wex et al. 2000 ; Wong et al. 2010 , see Sections  VIII.4 and VIII.12 , and also e.g.) .
Furthermore, to explain the significant misalignment of the spin-axis of the B-star companion from the orbital
angular momentum vector in the pulsar binary system PSR J0045 − - 7319, a large kick is needed too ( Kaspi et al. 1996 ; Wex 1998 ) .
Finally, there is some evidence for binaries being disrupted in the second SN, thereby explaining the
observations of isolated mildly recycled radio pulsars with similar properties to the first-born NS in DNS systems ( Lorimer et al. 2004 ) .
The velocities of such ejected NSs can be large even if the kick is small in case the former binary is
tight and disrupted due to a large amount of mass loss during the SN event ( Tauris & Takens 1998 ) . However, ultra-stripping prior to the second SN event
often prevents disruption due to mass loss and in these cases a large kick is needed to break up the system. 

### VI.5. Theoretical estimates of NS kick magnitudes

Kicks are associated with explosion asymmetries and may arise from non-radial hydrodynamic instabilities in the collapsing stellar core ( Blondin & Mezzacappa 2006 ; Scheck et al. 2006 ; Foglizzo et al. 2007 ; Marek & Janka 2009 ; Janka 2012 ; Janka 2013 ; Bruenn et al. 2014 ; Foglizzo et al. 2015 , i.e. neutrino-driven
convection and the standing accretion-shock instability,) . These instabilities are thought to lead to large-scale anisotropies
of the innermost SN ejecta, which interact gravitationally with the nascent NS and accelerate it on a timescale of several seconds ( Janka 2012 ; Wongwathanarat et al. 2013 , e.g.) . 

The magnitude of the kick velocity imparted on a newborn NS is difficult to calculate, even for a given progenitor star modelled
until the onset of the core collapse.
To predict the outcome of a stellar core collapse, O’Connor & Ott 2011 defined the compactness parameter of a stellar core at bounce by: 

 ξ M = M / M ⊙ R ⁡ ( M ) / 1000 ​ km , \xi_{\rm M}=\frac{M/M_{\odot}}{R(M)/1000\;{\rm km}}\,, 

 (21) 

where R ⁡ ( M ) R(M) is the radius of the coordinate enclosing mass, M M .
 Sukhbold & Woosley 2014 argued that ξ \xi at bounce is correlated with ξ \xi at the onset of core collapse, and hence the calculated
pre-SN density structures can help in forecasting the mapping between pre-SN (ideally even ZAMS for single stars) parameters and the outcome of a core collapse.
Therefore, to enable an estimate of the expected kicks, it is necessary to calculate stellar density structures at the end of
Case BB RLO evolutionary models ( Tauris et al. 2013 ; Tauris et al. 2015 ) . So far, however, these progenitor models have not been calculated self-consistently to
the very onset of core collapse (although see Moriya et al. 2017 , for a composite calculation model integrating two stellar evolution codes) . 

In Wongwathanarat et al. 2013 , a formula is presented (see their eq. 10) for estimating maximum kick magnitudes.
This formula is based on the assumption of a dipolar mass asymmetry, Δ ​ m \Delta m between the two hemispheres
which is a fraction (maybe at most 20–30%) of the total mass enclosed between the SN shock radius and the
proto-NS at the time the explosion sets in.
This mass can be determined numerically and scales with the mass in the postshock layer and thus, roughly, with the mass-infall rate
of the collapsing stellar core as a progenitor-core dependent quantity.
Moreover, the resulting kick depends on the shock velocity and thus on the explosion energy.
It is important to stress that the actual magnitude of a considered NS kick is somewhere between zero and the maximum value derived from the formulae of Wongwathanarat et al. 2013 ,
depending on the explosion asymmetry which develops stochastically. The statistical probability distribution of this stochastic asymmetry
must be determined using numerous explosion simulations.
Finally, it has been suggested that multi-dimensional effects in the convective burning shells at the onset of core collapse
can have an effect on the NS kick amplitude ( Burrows & Hayes 1996 ; Arnett & Meakin 2011 ; Müller et al. 2016 , e.g.) , i.e. pre-collapse asymmetries in the progenitor star could influence
the asymmetry parameter of Eq. ( 22 ) (see below) within the framework of the hydrodynamic kick mechanism. 

 Figure 15.— Dependence of the estimated NS kick magnitude, w w on the SN explosion energy, E exp E_{\rm exp} and the momentum-asymmetry parameter, α ej \alpha_{\rm ej} , see Eq. ( 22 ).
Hyperbolas for constant values of w = 50 ,  200 ,  500 ,  1000 w=50,\,200,\,500,\,1000 and 1500 ​ km ​ s − 1 1500\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} are shown. 

Recently, Janka 2017 summarized the current theoretical understanding of how NS kicks come about using the gravitational tug-boat mechanism
in asymmetric neutrino-driven core-collapse SN explosions.
He derived a simple proportionality between the kick velocity, w w , and the energy of the explosion, E exp E_{\rm exp} : 

 w = 211 ​ km ​ s − 1 ​ C ​ ( α ej 0.1 ) ​ ( E exp 10 51 ​ erg ) ​ ( M NS 1.5 ​ M ⊙ ) − 1 , w=211\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1}\;C\,\left(\frac{\alpha_{\rm ej}}{0.1}\right)\left(\frac{E_{\rm exp}}{10^{51}\;{\rm erg}}\right)\left(\frac{M_{\rm NS}}{1.5\;M_{\odot}}\right)^{-1}, 

 (22) 

where C C is a constant typically of order unity, M NS M_{\rm NS} is the (baryonic) NS mass, and α ej \alpha_{\rm ej} is the momentum-asymmetry parameter of the explosion ejecta, whose statistics needs to
be determined by hydrodynamic explosion modeling. The more asymmetric and more powerful the SN explosion is, the larger the NS kick can be (Fig.  15 ).
For example, according to Janka 2017 , and references therein, core-collapse SNe of relatively massive iron cores have E exp ≃ 3 × 10 50 − 2.5 × 10 51 ​ erg E_{\rm exp}\simeq 3\times 10^{50}-2.5\times 10^{51}\;{\rm erg} and α ej = 0 − 0.33 \alpha_{\rm ej}=0-0.33 ,
yielding kick values in a broad range between w = 0 − 1000 ​ km ​ s − 1 w=0-1000\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} , whereas progenitors of electron capture SNe (EC SNe)
and low-mass iron core-collapse SNe (Fe CCSNe) — often relevant for ultra-stripped SNe, see below — typically
have E exp ≃ 10 50 ​ erg E_{\rm exp}\simeq 10^{50}\;{\rm erg} and α ej ≲ 0.03 \alpha_{\rm ej}\lesssim 0.03 , resulting in very small kicks of w ≤ 10 ​ km ​ s − 1 w\leq 10\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} . 

We now continue discussing the kicks expected for ultra-stripped SNe.
As we shall see in Section  VIII , the possibility of such small kicks is indeed in fine agreement with constraints from many observed DNS systems. 

### VI.5.1 Ultra-stripped SN kicks

The concept of ultra-stripped SNe was recently addressed in Tauris et al. 2013 ; Tauris et al. 2015 . These are SNe where a naked helium star experiences
extreme stripping by a close-orbit compact object prior to its explosion. Ultra-stripped SNe are therefore
particularly relevant for the second SN in DNS systems (Fig.  1 ).
Their expected optical signatures are compatible with observations of rapidly decaying SN light curves, such as SN 2005ek ( Drout et al. 2013 ; Tauris et al. 2013 ; Moriya et al. 2017 ) . 

The flavour of ultra-stripped SNe can be either an EC SN, from a collapsing ONeMg core ( Nomoto 1987 ) , or an Fe CCSN.
The momentum kick imparted on a newborn NS via an EC SN is always expected to be small.
This follows from detailed simulations which imply: i) explosion energies significantly smaller than those inferred for
standard Fe CCSNe ( Kitaura et al. 2006 ; Dessart et al. 2006 ) ; and ii) short timescales to revive the stalled SN shock,
compared to the timescales of the non-radial hydrodynamic instabilities which are required to produce strong
anisotropies, e.g. Podsiadlowski et al. 2004 ; Janka 2012 .
Simulations of EC SNe by Kitaura et al. 2006 ; Dessart et al. 2006 predict explosion energies of about (or even less than) 10 50 ​ erg 10^{50}\;{\rm erg} , and
thus most likely kick velocities below 50 ​ km ​ s − 1 50\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} (Fig.  15 ). 

Whereas Fe CCSNe are certainly able to produce large kicks ( Janka 2017 , e.g.) , small NS kicks have been suggested to originate from
CCSNe with small iron cores ( Podsiadlowski et al. 2004 , and references therein) . For such small Fe CCSNe the situation is similar to that of EC SNe.
In both cases, SN simulations ( Gessner & Janka 2017 ) suggest fast explosions where
non-radial hydrodynamical instabilities (convectively driven or from the standing accretion shock) are unable to grow, leading to somewhat small kick velocities. 

Using binary stellar evolution arguments, Tauris et al. 2015 identified two factors which also imply that in ultra-stripped SNe the NS kicks may by small.
Firstly, from their modelling of the progenitor stars of ultra-stripped SNe, they have demonstrated that the expected amount of ejecta is extremely small ( ∼ 0.1 ​ M ⊙ \sim\!0.1\;M_{\odot} )
compared to standard SN explosions in which several M ⊙ M_{\odot} of material is ejected. This may likely lead to a weaker gravitational tug on the proto-NS ( Janka 2012 ; Janka 2017 , caused by asphericity of the
ejecta, e.g.) and thus a small kick.
Secondly, the binding energies of the envelopes of their final progenitor star models are often only a few 10 49 ​ erg 10^{49}\;{\rm erg} , such that even a weak outgoing shock can quickly
lead to their ejection, potentially before large anisotropies can build up. 

To mimic the outcome, but avoiding detailed binary stellar evolution calculations of Case BB RLO, Suwa et al. 2015 performed axisymmetric hydrodynamical simulations
of neutrino-driven explosions of ultra-stripped Fe CCSNe using the stellar evolution outcomes of single, evolved CO-stars.
All their models exhibited successful explosions driven by neutrino heating. Their diagnostic explosion energy, ejecta mass and nickel mass, were typically
 10 50 ​ erg 10^{50}\;{\rm erg} , 0.1 ​ M ⊙ 0.1\;M_{\odot} and 0.01 ​ M ⊙ 0.01\;M_{\odot} , respectively, i.e. compatible with observations of rapidly decaying
light curves such as SN 2005ek ( Drout et al. 2013 ; Tauris et al. 2013 ) . Moreover, their calculated kick velocities were typically less than 50 ​ km ​ s − 1 50\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} ,
and sometimes even below ∼ 10 ​ km ​ s − 1 \sim\!10\;{\rm km\,s}^{-1} , in agreement with the simulations discussed in Janka 2017 and references therein. 

### VI.6. Relation between NS mass and SN kick?

While the binary stellar evolution arguments of Tauris et al. 2015 for small kicks hold best for the lowest mass NSs formed in their models,
it is possible, in principle, that even rather massive NSs are produced from ultra-stripped progenitors, provided that the whole (most of the) metal core collapses.
Indeed, Tauris et al. 2015 predict that ultra-stripped SNe may potentially produce young NSs in DNS systems with a mass within the entire range 1.10 − 1.80 ​ M ⊙ 1.10-1.80\;M_{\odot} .
On the other hand, it is also expected that more massive pre-SN metal cores produce larger iron cores and thus more “normal” Fe CCSNe with larger explosion
energies and therefore larger kicks. 

### VI.6.1 Theoretical arguments for a M NS − w M_{\rm NS}-w relation

While the relation in Eq. ( 22 ) is supported by numerical SN simulations ( Janka 2017 , see references discussed by) , a dependence of the NS kick on the total SN ejecta mass or NS mass
is more indirect and enters through possible correlations of these quantities with E exp E_{\rm exp} . Janka 2017 provides arguments why collapse events of stellar cores with low compactness
(which are associated with EC SNe, low-mass Fe CCSNe, and ultra-stripped SNe with small metal cores) can be expected to produce NSs with considerably smaller kicks than SNe from more ordinary iron cores.
Since stellar cores with low compactness also typically produce lower-mass NSs ( Ugliano et al. 2012 ; Ertl et al. 2016 ; Sukhbold et al. 2016 , see) , a relation between SN kick and NS mass seems plausible.
We note that although in Eq. ( 22 ) w w appears to scale inversely proportionally to M NS M_{\rm NS} , the values of
 E exp E_{\rm exp} and α ej \alpha_{\rm ej} are systematically smaller for explosions leading to small values of M NS M_{\rm NS} , such that
a correlation between w w and M NS M_{\rm NS} is indeed expected in general. 

 Figure 16.— Kick velocity as a function of NS mass for the second SN explosion in the observed DNS systems (Table  7 ).
For each DNS system, we applied two distance models, cf. Section  II.2 . For clarity, the pink triangles are plotted systematically with an artificial
mass offset of + 0.002 ​ M ⊙ +0.002\;M_{\odot} .
The more massive NSs (PSR B1534+12 and B1913+16) seem to have received significantly larger kicks compared to the low-mass NS systems
(see Sections  VI.6 , VIII.4 and VIII.12 ).
